62em){.__m__-R2muqssm{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:58.333333333333336%;--col-max-width:58.333333333333336%;}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-R2muqssm"><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Our threat hunters have been busy searching for abuse of the recently-released zero-day remote code execution bug in Microsoft Office (<code>CVE-2022-30190</code>). As part of their investigations, they found evidence of a threat actor hosting malicious payloads on what appears to be an <code>Australian VOIP telecommunications provider</code> with a presence in the <code>South Pacific nation of Palau</code>.</p><p>Further analysis indicated that targets in <code>Palau</code> were sent malicious documents that, when opened, exploited this vulnerability, causing victim computers to contact the provider’s website, download and execute the malware, and subsequently become infected.</p><p><strong>Key Observations</strong></p><p>This threat was a complex multi-stage operation utilizing <code>LOLBAS</code> (Living off the Land Binaries And Scripts), which allowed the attacker to initialize the attack using the <code>CVE-2022-30190</code> vulnerability within the <code>Microsoft Support Diagnostic Tool</code>. This vulnerability enables threat actors to run malicious code without the user downloading an executable to their machine which might be detected by endpoint detection.</p><p>Multiple stages of this malware were signed with a legitimate company certificate to add additional legitimacy and minimize the chance of detection.</p><p><strong>First stage</strong></p><p>The compromised website, as pictured in the screenshot below, was used to host <code>robots.txt</code> which is an executable which was disguised as “robots.txt”. We believe the name was used to conceal itself from detection if found in network logs. Using the Diagnostics Troubleshooting Wizard (<code>msdt.exe</code>), this file “robots.txt” was downloaded and saved as the file (<code>Sihost.exe</code>) and then executed.</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Second Stage, Sihost.exe</strong></p><p>When the renamed “robots.txt” – “Sihost.exe” – was executed by msdt.exe it downloaded the second stage of the attack which was a loader with the hash <code>b63fbf80351b3480c62a6a5158334ec8e91fecd057f6c19e4b4dd3febaa9d447</code>. This executable was then used to download and decrypt the third stage of the attack, an encrypted file stored as ‘<code>favicon.svg</code>’ on the same web server.</p><p><strong>Third stage, favicon.svg</strong></p><p>After this file has been decrypted, it is used to download the fourth stage of the attack from <code>palau.voipstelecom.com[.]au.</code> These files are named <code>Sevntx64.exe</code> and <code>Sevntx.lnk</code>, which are then executed on the victims’ machine.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" about information stealers, Lumma Stealer, without a doubt, has become the notorious icon of this landscape. Not only could it count itself among the most sophisticated, technically advanced, and widespread stealers-as-a-service in the world, but it was also described in a variety of blog posts from basically everyone in the industry, including us.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this analysis, we describe a new variant of Lumma Stealer that we call Remus, a new x64 build which we suspected might occur in the foreseeable future after the doxxing of Lumma authors from late August to October 2025. And the future is here.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eRemus brings the same stealing arsenal to the table as we already know from Lumma, capable of stealing stored browser passwords, cookies, cryptocurrency, and much more. However, rather than revisiting Lumma's well-documented capabilities, we focus on the remarkable resemblance between Remus and Lumma, as well as the new techniques Remus introduces, including the use of EtherHiding to resolve C2s, replacing the traditional use of Steam and Telegram dead drop resolvers, and additional anti-analysis checks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attribution of Remus to Lumma is many-fold and described thoroughly throughout the blog post. The main indicators, to name a few, are the use of the same string obfuscation technique, AntiVM checks, direct syscall/sysenter handling, indirect control flow obfuscation, and, most importantly, an almost identical approach to bypassing AppBound Encryption, which we’ve only seen used explicitly by Lumma to date.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith that said, we can still see active Lumma campaigns all around the world, which makes Remus not a replacement, but a continuous evolution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDevelopment timeline\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore diving into the technical details, it is worth noting that during our hunting efforts, we came across several test samples, which even carry a \u003ccode\u003etestbuild\u003c/code\u003e label embedded directly in the binaries (see Figure 1). We are internally referencing these builds as Tenzor (based on encrypted strings). Many of the techniques and design patterns we encountered in Remus closely mirrored those we had already seen in Lumma Stealer. The main difference is that Lumma was 32-bit while Remus is 64-bit, which naturally introduced some variations. Yet, when we looked past the 32-bit versus 64-bit differences and stripped away the obfuscation layers, what remained was strikingly familiar.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the following subsections, we highlight the most compelling evidence of a direct connection between Remus and Lumma. Note that this is not an exhaustive list. The overlaps are far more numerous, but we selected just a few that we believe speak for themselves.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eForgotten dead drop resolver and string obfuscation\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor a malware researcher, strings are among the most valuable resources during reverse engineering, and in the case of attributing Remus to Lumma, they proved no different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth Remus and Lumma use a virtually identical mechanism for string obfuscation. The encrypted string is first assembled on the stack using a series of various forms of the \u003ccode\u003emov\u003c/code\u003e instruction, followed by a decryption loop that transforms the data byte by byte. The transformation is often further obscured by MBA (Mixed Boolean-Arithmetic) obfuscation and is either inlined or placed in a standalone function. In any case, each string is protected using a unique transformation, making it very difficult to build a universal static decryptor. As a result, emulating the decryption loops is likely the most effective approach for dealing with encrypted strings in both Remus and Lumma.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=gXkouPhN 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=VYItjdSJ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":760},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=RSIG-gtA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Sx7JfLS1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":578},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Kc1NFRAj 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=BYluRXHH 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":448}],"caption":"Figure 4: Decryption of the string “Processes.txt” in Remus (left) and Lumma (right). We are aware that the compilers themselves can emit \u003ccode\u003enop\u003c/code\u003e paddings. Still, in both Remus and Lumma, these sequences go beyond the typical, making it yet another indicator linking the two together.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter decrypting all strings in both Remus and Lumma and comparing them side by side, we found roughly 100 completely identical strings. Most of these, however, could be attributed to virtually any information stealer. This alone would already be a strong indicator.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHowever, what ultimately confirmed our theory was shifting the string comparison from Lumma vs. Remus to Lumma vs. Tenzor, as Tenzor effectively acts as a bridge between the other two – many of the strings we had previously matched only semantically between Remus and Lumma were still present in Tenzor in their original, unmodified form, directly matching Lumma’s. Reference sample: 0683f353cf3e101f721f1658e2a554ff7888ff9f2c32e23ceb3d23876864a264.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_07_0.jpg.webp?itok=g_hf2nx3","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch3\u003eApplication-bound encryption bypass\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnother very strong indicator tying Remus and Lumma together is the way they bypass Application-Bound Encryption (ABE), as both employ a highly specific technique that, until now, we have only observed and have been actively tracking in Lumma Stealer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe technique involves injecting a very short shellcode (less than 100 bytes) into the browser process to decrypt the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e. However, unlike most techniques relying on injection, Lumma as well as Remus do not use this shellcode to call \u003ccode\u003eIElevator::Decrypt\u003c/code\u003e with the key obtained from the \u003ccode\u003eLocal State\u003c/code\u003e file (the \u003ccode\u003e[“os_crypt”][“app_bound_encrypted_key”]\u003c/code\u003e JSON field). Instead, it locates the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly in the browser’s process memory, where it is stored in an encrypted form protected by \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. It then calls the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e with the same flag from within the browser’s context to decrypt it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is important to note that this in-memory encrypted form of the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e is protected with a \u003cstrong\u003edifferent key\u003c/strong\u003e than the one used to protect the key on disk – it is a separate protection layer that Chromium browsers use to safeguard the key while it resides in memory at runtime. However, since the key is protected with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag, decryption can only be performed by the same process that encrypted it, which is precisely why injection into the browser process is necessary in this specific bypass. For the curious, we discussed this topic in more detail in our recent\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eVoidStealer blog post\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom this point on, we will walk through the ABE bypass as implemented in Remus, noting any differences from Lumma’s implementation where applicable. Where no differences are explicitly mentioned, the two implementations work either identically or very similarly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo how does Remus find the protected \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e? It begins by walking the browser’s PEB module list, looking for two modules in a single pass: \u003ccode\u003edpapi.dll\u003c/code\u003e and the browser DLL (e.g., \u003ccode\u003echrome.dll\u003c/code\u003e).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen \u003ccode\u003edpapi.dll\u003c/code\u003e is found, Remus manually parses its PE export directory, reading the DOS header, PE header, and export table through repeated \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e syscalls. For each exported function name, it computes a seeded CRC32 hash and compares it against the pre-computed hash\u0026nbsp;corresponding to\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e. Essentially, a typical API-hashing, but in a remote process. While their implementations differ in some details, mostly as a natural consequence of the 32-bit vs 64-bit architecture, the overall design is strikingly similar.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne of the first things both do upon execution is that they enumerate all \u003ccode\u003eNt\u003c/code\u003e-prefixed exports from \u003ccode\u003entdll.dll\u003c/code\u003e and build a lookup table mapping their name hashes to the corresponding Syscall Service Numbers (SSNs). Lumma uses it specifically when interacting with 64-bit browsers (during the ABE bypass) to call native 64-bit \u003ccode\u003entdll.dll\u003c/code\u003e functions that have no NtWow64 equivalents – namely \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateThreadEx\u003c/code\u003e to start the injected thread, \u003ccode\u003eNtFreeVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e for clean up, and \u003ccode\u003eNtQueryVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e to query the browser’s memory layout. The remaining cross-process operations, such as memory reads, memory allocations, and shellcode writes, are handled through standard NtWow64 sysenter calls (\u003ccode\u003eNtWow64ReadVirtualMemory64\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eNtWow64AllocateVirtualMemory64\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003eNtWow64WriteVirtualMemory64\u003c/code\u003e). Since Heaven’s Gate is inherently a WoW64 technique applicable only to 32-bit processes running on 64-bit systems, and Remus is a 64-bit binary, it has no equivalent. Remus simply uses direct syscalls for all these operations.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eShared code patterns\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnother striking similarity is the layout of many functions, which shows a remarkable resemblance and, in some cases, is nearly identical (if we disregard the obfuscation and differences caused by 32-bit vs 64-bit code). While there are many such functions, we highlight two concrete examples. The first is the heap allocation wrapper, which Remus and Lumma implement in a virtually identical manner, as illustrated in Figures\u0026nbsp;24 and 25. Both Remus and Lumma begin executing their malicious logic directly from the PE entry point (the\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003estart\u003c/code\u003e\u0026nbsp;function), with no runtime initialization whatsoever. In a typical C/C++ binary, the entry point calls the CRT startup routine, which sets up the heap, initializes global variables, registers exception handlers, processes the command line, and invokes static constructors before eventually calling\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003emain\u003c/code\u003e. However, neither Remus nor Lumma follows this pattern. Instead, both jump directly from the entry point into their core logic: resolving modules and APIs, performing anti-analysis checks, initializing C2 communication, and exfiltrating data, ending with a direct call to \u003ccode\u003eExitProcess(0)\u003c/code\u003e. This suggests both were compiled without linking to the standard C runtime, likely a deliberate choice to minimize the binary size and reduce unnecessary dependencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe structural similarities extend further. Both binaries share the same section layout, with each section serving an identical semantic role. Even the C2 configuration follows the same pattern: in both cases, the encrypted blob, key, and nonce are statically embedded in the \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e section and decrypted at runtime using ChaCha20.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo summarize, we strongly believe the degree of similarity between the two codebases is too deliberate to be a coincidence, pointing to a single, shared origin.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eIndirect control flow obfuscation\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFinally, both Remus and Lumma make use of control flow obfuscation by replacing direct jumps with indirect ones, where the target is read from an offset stored in the \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e section. As shown in Figure 28, this technique takes several forms. In its simplest case, the target address is resolved by a single pointer dereference (highlighted in yellow). In more complex cases, it extends to a full jump table, where a computed index selects among several target addresses stored consecutively in the \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e section (highlighted in orange). Lastly, in some cases, the target address is first loaded into a register (highlighted in blue) and then jumped to via a register-based indirect jump (highlighted in pink).\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=yw52wiVm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=IzpQtf-7 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":526},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=p9HiY3wT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=SsanKuYo 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":400},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=Vkmug0fX 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=nkn_NNA0 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":310}],"caption":"Figure 28: Randomly selected disassembly code from Remus (left) and Lumma (right) highlighting the different forms of the discussed indirect control flow obfuscation.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=Vkmug0fX","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eAlthough functionally equivalent to normal jumps, the indirection\u0026nbsp;complicates the control flow analysis in two ways. First,\u0026nbsp;some of the indirect jump targets cannot be statically determined (for example, when the register holding the target address can be set in multiple different preceding basic blocks, each potentially loading a different target), leaving the control flow graph incomplete as the connections between basic blocks are lost. Second, some of these orphaned blocks end up being misidentified as standalone functions, further disrupting function boundary detection and leading to a broken, incomplete decompiler output. Although removing the obfuscation entirely requires more effort, the IDA’s decompiler output can be significantly improved by marking the referenced offsets as constants. For those interested, we covered this trick in our \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/defeating-aurastealer-obfuscation\"\u003eAuraStealer blog post\u003c/a\u003e, along with other approaches for tackling control flow obfuscation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat’s new in Remus\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHaving covered the key similarities between Remus and Lumma, let’s now focus on what is new in Remus. The core architecture is clearly inherited from Lumma. Still, several changes go beyond a straightforward 32-bit-to-64-bit port. These range from refactored strings and more thorough device fingerprinting to a different exfiltration archive format, a switch from FNV-1a to CRC32 for API hashing, and an overhauled C2 communication that has been restructured to deliver dynamic configurations incrementally rather than in a single large blob. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most significant changes, however, are the replacement of traditional Steam/Telegram dead drop resolvers with blockchain-based C2 resolution using EtherHiding, a persistence technique allowing attackers to store malware-related data (such as domains, payloads, encryption keys, among others) on blockchain as a smart contract, and the introduction of additional anti-analysis checks.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDead drop resolvers via EtherHiding\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth Remus and Lumma employ dead drop resolvers, a mechanism in which the malware does not contact its C2 server directly but instead retrieves the C2 address at runtime from an intermediary hosted on a legitimate platform. This makes the infrastructure significantly more resilient, as if a C2 domain is taken down, the dead drop can be adjusted to point to a new server without the need to distribute an updated binary. Moreover, the dead drop URLs typically reside on well-known, high-reputation services, making them more difficult for security vendors to block without collateral damage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhere the two differ is in the choice of platform. Lumma relies on Steam profiles and Telegram channels, typically encoding C2 URLs using ROT-15, while Remus goes a step further, replacing these with Ethereum smart contracts. At runtime, it sends an \u003ccode\u003eeth_call\u003c/code\u003e JSON-RPC request to a hardcoded contract address via a public RPC endpoint and extracts the C2 URL from the hex-encoded response. Because blockchain data is decentralized and immutable, there is no platform operator to report abuse to, making the dead-drop effectively immune to takedowns.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJust like Lumma’s Steam profile URL, Remus stores the smart contract address separately from the static C2 configuration, as a stack-encrypted string. Notably, after the JSON response is retrieved, the C2 address is, at least for now, merely hex-encoded in the \u003ccode\u003eresult\u003c/code\u003e field (no other obfuscation, such as ROT-15 in case of Lumma, is applied).\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=aykiX20l 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=pL76kfRZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":670},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=YSc7ZWVs 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=Y3V3btE1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":509},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=FFY3enyR 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=mSV0E7oS 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":395}],"caption":"Figure 30: Remus resolving a C2 using EtherHiding. If either check fails, the malware silently terminates via \u003ccode\u003eExitProcess(0)\u003c/code\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first check targets sandbox and analysis tool DLLs. We focused on various similarities and differences between Lumma and Remus, thoroughly describing the technical aspects of both. need Photoshop for a side project. The subscription is expensive. You search for a free Mac download, land on a site that looks good enough, and within an hour, the app is running. A few weeks later, your Mac is hot even when you're not doing much. The fans stay loud. The battery drains fast. Activity Monitor shows a process with a name you do not recognize, chewing through CPU in the background. That \"free\" app may have cost you your machine's processing power, your saved passwords, your browser sessions or access to the files on your disk. That trade is more common than many Mac users think.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIn the first 48 hours after we began tracking one recent cracked software distribution wave, our protections blocked about 108,000 attempts to launch these applications on macOS systems.\u003c/strong\u003e This is a count of blocked launch attempts recorded by one of our shields, not unique devices. Of those detections, 58% came from Macs still supported by Apple, while 42% came from systems that no longer receive Apple's current support. The pattern is already clear: even for MacOS, cracked apps are still a practical delivery channel for cryptominers, infostealers, adware and long-term persistence.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe signature that keeps showing up\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOpen enough cracked Mac apps and you start to see recurring patterns. One of the patterns we see quite often is a string left by the TNT group:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e.TNT - why join the navy if you can be a pirate\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStill, this story will not be about them but rather the whole distribution pipeline around them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA crack may start as a modified version of a legitimate app with license checks removed. After that, it often moves through mirror sites, torrent uploads, forum reposts, Telegram channels and re-packed archives. Every handoff is another chance to add something extra to the file. That is the real problem. Users rarely download a package from a trusted source. They are downloading whatever the last person in the chain decided to upload.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy people still download cracked Mac apps\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCost is one of the obvious reasons. A subscription can feel absurd when you need an app for one class, one freelance project or one weekend task. There is another reason too: old hardware. Plenty of people still use older Macs that work well enough for everyday tasks, but no longer run the latest macOS release. Once that happens, the legitimate software market starts shrinking around them. New versions no longer support old releases; official downloads for old versions get harder to find and compatibility drops. The machine still works, but the software ecosystem has moved on.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly when cracked software starts to look practical. It promises a version that runs on old hardware, costs nothing and solves the problem immediately. What it does not say is that older Macs are often already at a disadvantage. They may miss newer security features and recent patches. If the installation also tells the user to weaken built-in protections, the system becomes much easier to abuse.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow cracked apps reach users\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCracked macOS software spreads through a loose supply chain. Some files come from dedicated download sites that look almost like real app stores. Others spread through torrent trackers, warez forums, Telegram channels, Discord communities and file-sharing links posted on SEO-driven \"download\" pages. The packaging changes, the mirrors change and the hostnames change. The model stays the same.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe important point is not which site was involved. It is that every re-upload, re-zip and re-pack creates another opportunity to insert a payload. The original crack may be clean, but the version on a mirror site may already tell a different story; the torrent may include an extra installer, the archive posted to a chat channel may have been modified again. There is usually no reliable way for a typical user to tell the difference before running it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=VBfED1MK 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=XxWeLl0- 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":908,"height":854},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=Vb6lPM6t 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=KcYWsV5C 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":837},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=_clBtlLq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=fi2aKjgx 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":649}],"caption":"Every step between the original crack and the final download is an opportunity to add malware or unwanted software. MacOS includes several protections that are meant to stop exactly this kind of software from running freely. Gatekeeper helps block apps from unidentified developers. Notarization checks software distributed outside the App Store for known malicious content. System Integrity Protection, or SIP, helps prevent changes to critical parts of the system. Permission controls limit what apps can read and modify. And cracked software install guides often tell users to work around those protections step by step.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTypical instructions often include:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eae2da4be03f3a2b670f4a813338399f9\"\u003eDisable Gatekeeper so unsigned apps can data-list-item-id=\"ef4a9d11e409c539f6414805c6023c5d8\"\u003eLaunch the crack tool with administrator privileges.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli If an unsigned binary gets root privileges and Full Disk Access on a system with weakened protections, it has significantly more room to do real damage. On older Macs, the risk is even worse. When the OS is already behind on security updates, turning off the protections that remain can leave the system exposed for months.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat actually gets bundled with these downloads\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eUnfortunately, these payloads are a bit like a box of chocolates; you never know what you're gonna get. We tried to go through stats to identify some main types of these unwanted payloads.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed1f60a74a2f7183221b82c6518f4a758\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCryptominers\u003c/strong\u003e, such as XMRig, aim to use computer resources to mine cryptocurrency. Thus, besides the original app, the Mac will also do a second job in the background, usually resulting in high CPU usage, loud fans and significantly reduced battery life.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee96f909440c5a529a92515c4c36aebfc\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfoStealers\u003c/strong\u003e, on the other hand, are trying to stay under the radar, collect various data on the device and send it to the control server. Usually, they are after saved passwords, cookies, session tokens, wallet data or documents as these can be monetized quickly. Remember that full disk access in step 4? That one made it a whole lot easier.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecff0fdd0b1b2cd2b26f1850f4425166b\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBackdoors\u003c/strong\u003e are usually trying to stay low and provide persistent access to the computer, be it to deploy further malware or use the computer as a staging point for future actions (e.g., email campaign). On macOS, this usually means LaunchAgents or Launch Daemons that run automatically after rebooting.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efc64564b7278b592130a283c2184891b\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAdware and Browser Hijackers\u003c/strong\u003e are less dramatic than previous categories but still annoying, nonetheless. They may redirect your search, inject ads, change browser settings and commit affiliate fraud (e.g., injecting their affiliate links) - as the monetization relies on abuse of (semi-)legitimate internet infrastructure; these are the easiest ways to monetize. To be clear, the point is not that every cracked app is booby-trapped with these payloads. Unfortunately, obtaining cracked software may require you to trust an anonymous supply chain with system-level access to your systems, with limited options to verify what you got.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Mac myth still helps to keep this pipeline alive\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA lot of people still carry some version of the old belief that Macs do not really get malware. That idea was always overstated, as this usually relied on Macs being a smaller market for malware authors than the Windows ecosystem. Today, with many threats going multi-platform, this idea is actively dangerous.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile macOS has its own built-in protections that manage to provide some level of protection, they still rely on the user installing the software from trusted sources and crucially (but unsurprisingly), the user must not turn them off to have any effect. The moment a user starts disabling Gatekeeper, turning off SIP and granting broad permissions to unsigned binaries, the system is left exposed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis combination makes cracked software on macOS quite an attractive delivery method. Leading the user to disable their AV is one of the oldest tricks in the book, but this one takes it a step further by implicitly banking on a still-popular myth to avoid raising suspicion.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIs there a way back?\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e36f761d5067b1f3b97fcb41250f533d3\"\u003eRe-enable SIP in Recovery Mode:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecsrutil enable\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e13ce9ed1846fa4c90cd5595cc3c40d05\"\u003eThen re-enable Gatekeeper in Terminal:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003esudo spctl --master-enable\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3f587d06dd59561a968a5024f26dcf20\"\u003eCheck common persistence locations. Unfamiliar .plist files or oddly named helper items are worth investigating.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e~/Library/LaunchAgents/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e/Library/LaunchAgents/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e/Library/LaunchDaemons/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e~/Library/Application Support/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e844d0ecf4d5c86132be363b690f9a29c\"\u003eRun a full system scan using e.g., Avast for Mac or Norton Security for Mac. Running it on the \u003cem\u003eDownloads\u003c/em\u003e folder might not be enough.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ead808099598a77195a50d27149505dc1\"\u003eConsider changing important passwords. Start with email, banking, work accounts, cloud storage, and any password manager. This is especially crucial if the full system scan identified malware.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb0715b9167c2c31908d76fb610c0f80e\"\u003eWatch for follow-on activity. Look for login alerts, suspicious financial activity and unusual browser behavior that continue after removing the cracked app. Consider a clean install if the system was compromised for an extended period. The safest approach is a full macOS reinstall from Recovery Mode. Back up personal files (documents, photos — not applications), wipe the drive, reinstall macOS and restore only verified clean data.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe real price of \"free\"\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCracked software might sound attractive because it solves an immediate problem. It saves money. It keeps an old Mac useful for a little longer. It avoids a subscription that people don't want to pay. But the actual deal may look different once you include the hidden terms. Even when the original crack is benign, things may get tangled once you consider distribution, potentially including other middleman in the transaction, with each possibly vying for their share.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the goal is simply to get work done, the safer alternatives are boring but real: official older installers when vendors provide them, open-source alternatives, lower-cost one-time-purchase tools, student licensing or used hardware that can still run a supported macOS release.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOur role: Protection, not judgment\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs a company behind reputable antivirus solutions such as Avast, AVG and Norton, we want to be clear: we don't judge users by the software choices they make. We understand that people might turn to cracked software to solve their problems, be it tight budgets, old hardware that still works or needing an app for a short-term project. We get it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur job is not to be the software police.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur job is to protect you from threats that hide in the places where you might be looking for solutions.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen our detection systems flag a cracked app, it is not because we disapprove of your choice to use it. It is because we found something genuinely malicious bundled with it - a cryptominer, an infostealer, a backdoor or other malware that puts your system and data at risk.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf we block a cracked app, you can be confident that we detected something nasty lurking inside. Our goal is to let you make informed decisions about the software you install, with full knowledge of what risks come with it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFinal thought\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe quote that TNT and other groups stamp on cracked software, \"Why join the Navy if you can be a pirate?\", was about thinking differently. About building something new instead of following the established path. About creativity and independence. Using it to brand cracked software misses the point entirely. The real pirates in this story are usually not the users trying to save money or keep an old Mac useful for a little longer. They are the actors further up the chain: repackagers, mirror operators, uploaders and malware distributors who turned cracked software into a monetization engine.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is what makes this ecosystem work so well. The person downloading the app thinks they are taking a shortcut. They might be stepping into a supply chain built to extract value from them, whether that means mining cryptocurrency in the background, stealing credentials, hijacking browser traffic or keeping a foothold on the machine for later.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStill, the end decision is not ours to make.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you make your decision, step with care.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs you might eventually realize that you did not join the Navy. You did not become a pirate. 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design-8.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=92yiSrTr 2x","media":"(min-width: 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design-8.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=P3i6Rzvl 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design-8.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=utTKQv6n"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe deepfake story you think you know is incomplete.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen most people hear “deepfake,” they picture a fake face.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne important nuance: \u003cstrong\u003ea deepfake face does not automatically mean a scam\u003c/strong\u003e, and a scam does not even require a deepfake face at all. Visuals can be a useful clue, but \u003cstrong\u003eidentifying synthetic media is getting more complex\u003c/strong\u003e, and simplistic “looks fake” judgments are increasingly unreliable. That is why we lean on \u003cstrong\u003emultiple signals\u003c/strong\u003e, including audio analysis and transcript, to reason about intent and behavior.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eConnecting the term deepfake with deceit makes sense, it is the most visual part of the problem, and it has become a cultural shorthand for deception. But in real-world scam campaigns, the face is often just packaging. The engine is usually the message, including the script, the call to action, the pressure, and the promise.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat's why our detection starts where the persuasion lives: the content, especially the audio. Scam videos often reuse the same pitches, again and again, such as “guaranteed returns,” “limited-time access,” “you’ve been selected,” “act before it’s too late.” Even when the visuals look polished, even when the footage looks familiar, the audio can give away the intent.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs we continued our research on what makes a deepfake a scam, we started asking another question that changed what we looked for:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOnce a video is already labeled as scam content by our audio and content analysis, which faces keep showing up across those scam videos?\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe answer was not only “famous people.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt was actually a set of VIPs that do not exist.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy we looked for “VIPs” in scam video\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn scam ecosystems, repetition is rarely an accident. Criminals optimize what works, then scale it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo instead of asking “who is this person?” we asked “how often does this face appear across scam-labeled videos, and where does it show up?” If the same presenter is repeatedly used as a delivery vehicle for scam scripts, that recurrence becomes a signal of reuse, coordination, and industrialization.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo do that, our proof of concept takes scam-labeled videos, extracts faces, generates compact visual descriptors, then groups them by similarity. The most prevalent identities in that scam-labeled set become “VIPs,” meaning faces that appear unusually often across scam content. We use faces here as an additional \u003cstrong\u003esignal \u003c/strong\u003efor grouping and speed, to connect related scam videos and spot scale. It complements audio and transcript analysis, it is not the primary factor deciding whether a video is a scam.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe expected the usual outcome: a list dominated by high-profile public figures that scammers frequently impersonate.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd yes, those faces are there. Public figures like Elon Musk have become recurring bait in investment-themed scams, giveaways, and “exclusive opportunity” narratives.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBut the most important finding was what appeared alongside the celebrity targets.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eVIPs that don’t exist\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSome of the most prevalent “presenters” in scam video are not real people.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThey look like ordinary talking-head hosts: friendly expression, neutral styling, camera-ready delivery. They speak clearly, they project confidence, and they often appear in exactly the kind of format people have learned to trust: a calm explainer, a “news-style” segment, a professional endorsement, a casual recommendation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBut they are AI avatars, synthetic presenters generated to be reused. To be clear, an AI avatar is not automatically a scam; what makes these specific presenters relevant is that we found them recurring inside videos already labeled as scams based on the message and behavior.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn general, proving that a person on screen is synthetic can require detailed and expensive analysis, things like lip-sync consistency, motion artifacts, and other forensic techniques. Our approach is different: we identify and aggregate recurring avatar identities from scam-labeled videos using lightweight visual descriptors and similarity grouping. That gives us a faster, cheaper way to recognize AI avatar presenters that are not yet “known,” but are already showing up frequently in the wild.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe are confident about this classification. These are not ambiguous “maybe enhanced” clips. They are AI avatar outputs, and we have also seen the supply chain behind them, including avatar creation services marketed openly on freelance marketplaces like Fiverr.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis matters because it changes the threat model.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you only think about deepfakes as “someone impersonating a celebrity,” you miss the bigger shift: scammers are increasingly manufacturing credibility without impersonating anyone at all.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat might sound like a minor distinction, but it changes the incentives.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e908326041bf0d633ca65ffb02170a711\"\u003eA celebrity deepfake creates obvious controversy and is easier for platforms and victims to challenge.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3d8efe40c9161bf5c06bb597444c608c\"\u003eA synthetic presenter has no “real person” attached, no victim to complain, and no public figure to debunk the content.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efe3688ea8aebb1aa6c655308f6223fda\"\u003eThe same synthetic presenter can be reused endlessly, across topics and languages, with minimal cost.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn short, these “VIPs” are not famous because they are recognized, they are VIPs because they are operational assets in scam production.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFrom celebrity deepfakes to avatar farm\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCelebrity impersonation is still here, and it will remain attractive. Reputation is a powerful shortcut to trust.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBut impersonation has trade-offs. It is high-profile, easier to report, and riskier for scammers. It can also be brittle, a single debunk can collapse a campaign.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSynthetic presenters reduce that risk. You do not have to mimic a specific person, you just have to look credible enough to keep someone listening for 20 seconds.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is why the “avatar farms” mental model fits.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eInstead of crafting a one-off forgery, scammers can run a production line:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ebb598c8a5f36ed5bf22c58a25cf5137d\"\u003eCreate or acquire a synthetic presenter that looks broadly trustworthy\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e037f34075f6fe1c6b325444d26aec9ad\"\u003eGenerate scripts at volume, tailored to whatever is trending\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9830311305c72262b00d5e0ed4706f87\"\u003eSwap voiceovers, captions, and visual overlays\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1829309715a175d81fbb484eaf94b1e0\"\u003ePublish, measure engagement, then publish again with minor variations\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat production line scales. It is designed for experimentation, iteration, and reach.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd it works because most scams do not require cinematic realism. They require momentum.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSynthetic trust, the new social engineering\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHumans are wired to treat faces and voices as social proof. A confident presenter can make a claim feel less risky, even when the claim is outrageous. A calm delivery can make urgency feel “reasonable.” A familiar format can make a stranger’s instructions feel like a normal next step.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the core idea behind synthetic trust.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI avatars are not trying to be memorable. They are trying to be acceptable. They are engineered to pass a quick gut-check, “this seems legitimate,” long enough to drive a click, a download, a payment, or a message.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor scammers, that is an upgrade. A synthetic presenter does not get tired. It does not demand a fee. It does not have a moral objection. It can be generated in many languages, in endless variations, and deployed in parallel across platforms.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou may still see celebrity bait at the top of the funnel. But underneath it, a lot of scam content is shifting toward manufactured presenters that are easier to scale.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe twist: sometimes the video is real and only the audio is weaponized\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is another reason audio-first detection matters: not every scam video relies on a fake face.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSometimes criminals take legitimate footage, a real interview, a real tutorial, a real product explainer, and overlay a scam pitch on top. The visuals can be authentic. The deception sits in the narration, the instructions, and the call to action.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis technique does two things for scammers:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee6d78ff507a6dd63751daeb5224bcc60\"\u003eIt reduces production cost, because they can recycle existing footage\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eae4e6dba3c1d54936aa4100bfd420af0\"\u003eIt reduces suspicion, because the visuals look “normal” at a glance\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf defenses focus only on “does the face look fake?”, this kind of repurposed content can slip through. That is why we treat the message as primary. Then, once content is labeled as scam, we look for the repeated visual infrastructure that carries those messages, including recurring AI avatar presenters.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA note on hired actors\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eNot every recurring presenter in scam content is synthetic. We have also seen scams that use hired actors or commissioned videos. Sometimes it is a human reading a script, sometimes it is a human clip later repurposed with different narration or overlays.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words: the “avatar farm” trend does not replace all old techniques. It sits alongside them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is also why we avoid simplistic rules like “this face is fake therefore it is a scam.” What matters is intent and behavior. Faces help us connect the dots faster as a signal, they are not the verdict.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003ePractical tips: how to protect yourself from scam videos\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf a video is pushing you toward money, logins, downloads, or urgent verification, treat the presenter as part of the packaging, not proof.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb375588bdd69b963a569da0423a5860d\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFollow the action, not the face.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eIf the video is urging urgency, secrecy, or immediate payment, pause. That pressure is often the scam.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecf36de53c2869ca44dd7de68b8dd6a10\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eVerify outside the platform.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eSearch for the claim from independent sources, not links in the description or comments.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea8e5ddb75021374558a5553272959836\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBe suspicious of “too smooth” certainty.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eScam scripts often sound confident, generic, and strangely frictionless, as if risk does not exist.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee5e84990c119d747dd4cb8566a61b415\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWatch for mismatch.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eIf the visuals feel unrelated to the pitch, or the audio feels pasted-on, treat it as a warning sign.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecd31fbfea4a6ecebbceff95dfbcc3458\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAvoid installs prompted by videos.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e“Install this to verify,” “download this viewer,” “run this command,” these are common routes into malware and credential theft.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e190568cd7906f183381254eeea433387\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUse protection tools.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cbr\u003eTools like Norton’s AI-powered Scam Protection and Avast Deepfake Guard can help spot what’s easy to miss, including deepfake audio in videos and scam signals across web, SMS and more.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe bottom line\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp lang=\"EN-US\"\u003eDeepfakes may get the headlines, but they are only part of the story. From impersonating real people to manufacturing trust at scale, faces, voices, scripts, formats, all of it can be generated, reused and optimized.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp lang=\"EN-US\"\u003eWhich means the real skill is not spotting what looks fake; it is recognizing how scams behave.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp lang=\"EN-US\"\u003eFollow the pressure. Question the promise. Verify outside the starts with a message that does not feel wrong.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou have a family trip coming up. You made the booking days or weeks ago, saved the confirmation and moved on with your life. Then WhatsApp pings.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe message says it is from the hotel’s Guest Relations team. It references the same property you booked. It mentions details that match your stay. It sounds like the kind of practical note you might expect before a trip, a small inconsistency, a quick verification, a routine check before arrival.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd that is exactly why it works.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a scam contains real details, it stops feeling like spam and starts feeling like customer service. The attacker does not need perfect writing. They do not need malware. They do not even need a particularly clever pretext. They just need enough truth to make the lie feel safe.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the pattern at the heart of what we are calling the \u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/blog/online-scams/reservation-hijacking-scam\" target=\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eReservation Hijack Scam\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe call it a \u003cstrong\u003eReservation Hijack Scam\u003c/strong\u003e because the attacker is not just spoofing a hotel or sending a generic phishing lure. They are hijacking the context around a real reservation and using it to make fraud feel like routine customer service.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the stronger versions of the attack, that goes further. Once hotel staff credentials are phished, the attacker may gain access to real hotel or partner accounts and contact guests from within legitimate workflows, for example through a booking platform account or hotel messaging environment. At that point, the scam is no longer only borrowing the hotel’s identity, it is actively impersonating the hotel through systems the guest already trusts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the shift that matters most. This is not just a travel-themed phishing lure dressed up with better wording. In the strongest cases, criminals can move from impersonating the hotel to operating through compromised hotel-side or partner workflows, contacting real travelers in the context of real reservations.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA scam category built on stolen and relayed trust\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost phishing is generic. It arrives with no context, so your brain treats it with suspicion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReservation Hijack scams work differently. They reflect real context back at the victim, the hotel name, the destination, the fact that there is a genuine booking, sometimes the stay window, sometimes even the exact amount due. That reflected truth becomes the bypass.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAdd a short fuse, usually 24 or 48 hours, and the attacker gets what they want most, fast compliance before the victim pauses, verifies or calls the property.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is not just a nicer phishing template. It is a different quality of deception.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/trustrelay1.jpg.webp?itok=Kgwa6dQ8 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/trustrelay1.jpg.webp?itok=7dqSf_MN 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":757,"height":1142},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/trustrelay1.jpg.webp?itok=CpU9djvb 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/trustrelay1.jpg.webp?itok=mdqbeqkf 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":757,"height":1142},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/trustrelay1.jpg.webp?itok=IlM0qBbY 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/trustrelay1.jpg.webp?itok=2K_yuDVQ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":1041}],"caption":"Scam message received by a victim using data from a real reservation","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/trustrelay1.jpg.webp?itok=IlM0qBbY","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eWhat we found\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur investigation points to two visible fronts in the same scam pattern.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first is the booking-platform lure. its core, this scam follows a simple pattern, even if the entry point can vary.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFirst, the attacker gains access to trust, typically by phishing hotel staff and stealing That is what makes the scam feel different from ordinary phishing. The victim is not chosen at random. They are contacted in the context of a real trip, with details that match an actual booking.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe level of personalization depends on how much information the attacker has. When they can access rich reservation data, such as the property name, stay dates, guest name, payment context or booking details, they can build a highly tailored spear phishing message that feels almost indistinguishable from routine customer service. When they only have limited data, such as an email address or phone number, the scam may look more generic, but it can still exploit hotel branding, urgency and the expectation of pre-trip communication.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat difference is visible in the lures themselves. In high-context variants, the message may name the guest, reference a specific stay date and amount, or lead to a payment page already populated with reservation details. In lower-context variants, the flow is far more generic and relies on the victim to fill in the missing details themselves.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the weaker version of the scam, the attacker uses that information to craft convincing messages from the outside, through WhatsApp, SMS or email lookalikes. In the stronger version, the attacker may message the guest from a real hotel or booking-related account they have taken over. The difference is important. The victim may not just see a plausible hotel message, they may see what looks like a legitimate continuation of their existing booking conversation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn one case we reviewed, the phishing message was delivered through a legitimate Booking.com partner communication thread after the accommodation partner account had been compromised. The malicious request was injected into an otherwise normal conversation, making the attack especially difficult to detect because it arrived from a trusted source rather than a spoofed sender. The follow-up warning from the hotel highlights the confusion and damage that can occur once a real partner account is abused:\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=V8RBsqNr 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=LcTJw8nF 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":843,"height":2069},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=Cv2U4dxO 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=0N5UdXba 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":843,"height":2069},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=K26Q5Psq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=yzKFVDXm 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":1693}],"caption":"Phishing injected into a legitimate Booking.com message thread","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=K26Q5Psq","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThen comes the message. It usually arrives over a fast, informal channel such as WhatsApp or SMS, and it is written to feel administrative rather than alarming. The goal is not to scare the victim, but to make the request feel routine, a payment issue, a booking confirmation, a verification step or an urgent action before arrival.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the examples we reviewed, the exact wording and format varied by platform, but the underlying pattern stayed consistent: the message borrowed hotel context, introduced a practical problem, and pushed the guest toward a verification or payment step that benefited the attacker.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom there, the victim is pushed into a controlled flow. That may be a fake guest portal, a spoofed payment page, or even a branded PDF that adds one more layer of credibility before the victim reaches the real theft stage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe theft happens when the traveler enters payment details, approves a transfer or otherwise complies with the fake verification process. At that point, the attacker has turned stolen trust into financial fraud.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd the damage may not stop there. Payment details can be reused, tested with small charges, sold or leveraged in follow-on scams. What begins as a believable hotel message can end in broader financial compromise.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is what makes this scam different. It is not just phishing with a travel theme. It is a workflow attack built on stolen context and relayed trust.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Cloudbeds path, step by step\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCloudbeds has publicly warned of an industry-wide phishing campaign targeting hospitality providers and described credential-phishing attacks impersonating Cloudbeds login pages as the root cause rather than a Cloudbeds platform breach.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat point matters especially for small and medium-sized hospitality businesses. Large travel brands usually have bigger security teams, more formalized controls and more mature fraud processes. Smaller properties often rely on lean teams, fast guest communication and a handful of operational platforms to keep everything moving. That makes them particularly vulnerable when attackers target employees directly with credential phishing.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attack chain we mapped is brutally efficient.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFirst, hotel staff are lured to fake login pages and their credentials are stolen. Then the attacker logs into the real management environment. From there, they can see future reservations, guest names, contact details, stay information and sometimes payment context. In some scenarios, that access may also extend beyond visibility. If the compromised hotel workflow is connected to guest-facing messaging on booking or hospitality platforms, the attacker may be able to contact customers from the property’s legitimate account presence. That makes the fraud especially dangerous, because the message may arrive inside a channel the traveler already associates with their real reservation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the cases documented in our working material, some victims received SMS messages containing detailed reservation information and links that led to professionally styled PDFs impersonating hotel groups. Those PDFs pushed “payment verification” within 48 hours, using generic branding, urgency, and a prominent call to action to move the victim toward the real theft stage. The PDF step appears to function as a buffer, one more layer of legitimacy for the victim, and one more layer of indirection for scanners and filters to deal with. In some cases, the PDF itself was hosted on legitimate partner storage that had been hijacked, giving the lure another borrowed layer of trust before redirecting the victim onward.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=FsgHRmxW 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=wIKnhQ7r 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1012,"height":1094},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=v_a6UT7P 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=dlnsQLcq 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":962},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=nSE_MyF6 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=ykCCn1dc 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":746}],"caption":"Victim is redirected to typo-squatted domains designed to harvest card details, bank transfers or other payment information","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=nSE_MyF6","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eFrom there, the victim is redirected again, typically to typo-squatted domains designed to harvest card details, bank transfers or other payment information. Example domains documented in the shared material include frontdesk-reservation[.]com, frontdesk-online[.]biz, and hotel.form842987[.]digital.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOne scam, multiple surfaces\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the part many people miss.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe WhatsApp message, the SMS, the fake PDF, the lookalike payment page, the stolen card, these are not separate incidents. They are one pipeline. And that pipeline can include direct impersonation of the hotel itself. Sometimes the attacker stands outside the workflow and imitates the property. Sometimes the attacker steps inside a compromised hotel or booking-related account and speaks from a position the guest already trusts. Different surface, same scam family.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFirst, the attacker gets context. Next, they use that context to deliver a believable message through a channel people act on quickly. Then they move the victim into a verification flow that feels administrative, not dangerous. If payment details are entered, the harm does not stop at one fraudulent charge. Cards can be tested with small transactions, then used for larger purchases, then resold or retried later.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is what makes the scam so dangerous. The same fraud logic can be adapted to different platforms, different levels of stolen context, and different points in the guest journey.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy this matters now\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHospitality has become an unusually attractive environment for this kind of fraud.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHotels run on urgency, constant guest communication and high-trust operational workflows. Travelers expect messages about check-in, balances, room preferences, arrival timing, and identity verification. That makes it easier for attackers to hide malicious intent inside language that would look normal in almost any other context.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd when the attacker is working with real reservation data, or worse, using compromised hotel or booking-linked accounts to contact guests directly, the old consumer advice starts to bend. Checking the link still matters, but it is no longer enough on its own. The victim is not just being tricked by branding. They are being tricked by context.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat travelers should do\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf a hotel contacts you via Booking.com messaging, Airbnb, WhatsApp, SMS or email and asks you to verify payment details, do not tap the link. The same caution applies if the message appears to come from an existing reservation thread or from what looks like the hotel’s real account on a booking platform. Trust the booking, not the message.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGo to the booking site, hotel site, or official app yourself. If you need to call the property, use a trusted contact route from the original booking or the verified website, not the number or link inside the message.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you already entered payment details, assume compromise. Contact your bank, cancel the card, enable transaction alerts and watch for follow-on attempts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat hotels should learn from this\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe lesson for hospitality is not just “train staff better,” although that matters. It is that hotel software, messaging workflows, and guest communication tools now sit directly on the fraud path.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe hotel is no longer only a victim. In the eyes of the guest, it can become the face of the scam. In the worst cases, it can also become the voice of the scam, when attackers use compromised hotel or partner accounts to message guests as if they were legitimate staff.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat means account protection, phishing-resistant authentication, tighter controls around exports and guest messaging, anomaly detection and faster incident response are no longer nice-to-haves. They are part of brand protection.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBecause once attackers learn how to turn operational systems into trust amplifiers, they do not need to break in loudly. They can simply log in, relay stolen trust to the guest, and let the workflow do the rest.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOne final thought\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor years, the best advice on travel scams was simple: watch for bad grammar, generic messages, and suspicious links.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat advice still matters. But it belongs to an earlier phase of the problem.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe next phase looks like this: the message knows your booking, sounds plausible, and may arrive through a channel or account the traveler already trusts. That is the point where customer service and fraud begin to overlap.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the Reservation Hijack Scam.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the next part of this
