started with a lie. A sample walked into the lab wearing a Vidar label like a cheap suit two sizes too small. We pulled the threads, and the whole thing came apart. What fell out was a previously unknown information stealer we named \u003cstrong\u003eTorg Grabber\u003c/strong\u003e – 334 samples compiled over three months, a rapid evolution from Telegram dead drops through an encrypted TCP protocol nobody asked for, all the way to a production-grade REST API that worked like a Swiss watch dipped in poison. Over 40 operator tags pulled from the binaries – a mix of nicknames, date-encoded batch IDs, and confirmed Telegram user IDs – fingerprinted individual MaaS customers and confirmed what we already suspected: this was a builder-and-panel operation, crime as a service, infrastructure included. OSINT resolution of the numeric tags peeled back the curtain on eight operators as Telegram accounts buried up to their necks in the Russian cybercrime ecosystem. The bot tokens gave us the developer accounts behind the whole show. Nobody said crime doesn’t pay, but nobody said it doesn’t leave fingerprints either.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe misclassification that started It all\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe sample came in on a Tuesday in early February, wearing the name Vidar Stealer the way a pickpocket wears a priest’s collar. It didn’t fit. A 747 KB PE64 binary compiled with MinGW/GCC – when Vidar v17.9 is a 32-bit MSVC job that, as a colleague put it, “looks significantly different.” That was like saying a Great Dane looks significantly different from a Pekingese. You didn’t need a magnifying glass.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA deeper look turned up a self-identifying debug string – \"\u003ccode\u003egrabber v1.0\u003c/code\u003e\" – sitting in the binary like a monogrammed handkerchief left at a crime scene. The C2 protocol was all wrong for Vidar: REST API endpoints (/api/auth, /api/upload/chunk), ChaCha20 encryption, HMAC-SHA256 authentication. This wasn’t Vidar any more than I was the Queen of England.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe ran it against StealC v2 for good measure – similar endpoint patterns on the surface, the way all gin joints look alike from the sidewalk. Zero protocol overlap. Different endpoint structure, different communication model, different encryption. This was something new, something nobody had put a name to yet.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo we named it ourselves. \u003cstrong\u003eTorg Grabber\u003c/strong\u003e – “Torg” from its most-used C2 domain \u003ccode\u003etechnologytorg.com\u003c/code\u003e (Russian \u003cem\u003e“торг”\u003c/em\u003e = trade, marketplace – fitting for a business that trades in other people’s secrets), and “Grabber” from the malware’s own vocabulary (\u003ccode\u003eGRABBER_HOST, GRABBER_TAG, \"grabber v1.0\u003c/code\u003e\"). If a thing names itself, you ought to listen.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTimeline: Two months of rapid development\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThree hundred thirty-four samples. Three months. It was like watching a time-lapse film of a weed growing through concrete. The corpus told the story of a malware operation spinning up in real time, stumbling forward with the reckless confidence of a man who knows exactly what he’s doing and doesn’t much care who’s watching.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/torgrabber1.png.webp?itok=dh2ZSgT7 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/torgrabber1.png.webp?itok=IK04MJcE 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":840,"height":732},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/torgrabber1.png.webp?itok=RX35rvzp 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/torgrabber1.png.webp?itok=Zon4hCL8 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":840,"height":732},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/torgrabber1.png.webp?itok=PpNVP9jS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/torgrabber1.png.webp?itok=9EqNK0_N 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":601}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/torgrabber1.png.webp?itok=PpNVP9jS","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThree exfiltration mechanisms. Three ways to get the goods out. Each one a little smarter than the last, the way a burglar graduates from smashing windows to picking locks to making copies of the key.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePhase 1: Telegram Bot API (Dec 9–11, 2025)\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe earliest builds were small and nervous, the way a rookie’s hands shake on his first job. Compact little things, 270 to 313 KB, that grabbed credentials, stuffed them into a ZIP archive, and shipped the whole package to a private Telegram channel via\u0026nbsp;sendDocument. No encryption beyond TLS. A\u0026nbsp;User-Agent: TG header about as subtle as a neon sign in a funeral parlor. Five retry attempts, and if the ZIP upload choked, a fallback to plaintext\u0026nbsp;sendMessage – the digital equivalent of shouting your secrets across a crowded room.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwenty-five samples, eight bot tokens, eight private channels, each with two or three members. This was the prototype phase: fast to set up, zero infrastructure cost, and about as durable as a paper umbrella in a monsoon. Telegram can revoke tokens on a whim, and the API calls stick out in traffic logs like a tall man in a short crowd.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePhase 2: GRAB Binary TCP Protocol (Dec 17–20, 2025)\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFour samples. A narrow four-day window. This was the experimental phase – somebody had an idea, and they were going to see if it could walk before they taught it to run.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eInstead of HTTP, the malware opened a raw TCP socket to\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e*.tara.net.bd\u003c/code\u003e on port 50443 and spoke a custom binary protocol that had all the charm of a coded knock on a speakeasy door:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3d01df3606010b531fb2e83821bb7f96\"\u003eA 9-byte frame: 4-byte\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eGRAB\u003c/code\u003e magic, 1-byte type, 4-byte length\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0aa34faadca69a4d6fcee2220c2a3415\"\u003eChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD encryption with HMAC-SHA256 key derivation\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eec12b3e52f329ead2daead35c83d690c\"\u003eSHA-256 hash of the payload sent before the encrypted data\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0500a32b5824c04717745665cb222a44\"\u003e64 KB chunks with 5-second retry intervals\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe C2 hostnames were\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003ebk.tara.net.bd and\u0026nbsp;raketa.tara.net.bd\u003c/code\u003e – “raketa” being Russian for “rocket,” because even criminals have a sense of poetry. Both under a Bangladesh TLD but geolocated to Turkey, which made about as much geographic sense as a polar bear in Havana. The encryption key was hardcoded:\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e0123456789abcdef\u003c/code\u003e repeated. A test key. Development builds, still warm from the compiler.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eSee Appendix A, Figure A1\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTechnically sophisticated – proper AEAD, authenticated handshakes, chunked transfer. The kind of work that said the developer knew cryptography the way a safecracker knows tumblers. But it was abandoned after four builds. Running a custom TCP protocol at scale turned out to be harder than hiding behind Cloudflare, which is to say, harder than letting somebody else do the heavy lifting. Some lessons you only need to learn once.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePhase 3: REST API over HTTPS (Dec 18 onward)\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis was the production rig. The real thing. No more rehearsals.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe malware performs a JSON registration at\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e/api/auth\u003c/code\u003e, sending along a machine fingerprint – GPU, wallpaper hash, hardware ID, antivirus product – like a new inmate checking into prison with his personal effects. In return, it gets configuration, a session token, and sometimes shellcode, because some operators like to bring their own party favors. Then comes the upload: stolen data, chunked over HTTPS, ChaCha20-encrypted, routed through Cloudflare. Each request carries an HMAC-SHA256\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eX-Auth-Token\u003c/code\u003e header, because even thieves want to know they’re talking to the right fence.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eSee Appendix A, Figure A2\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOver two months, the binary fattened up from ~410 KB to ~747 KB, the way a confidence man’s wallet thickens over a good season. The additions were considerable: a 20 KB ChaCha20-encrypted Reflective DLL for bypassing Chrome 127+ App-Bound Encryption across 7 Chromium browsers via their IElevator COM interfaces; 20 direct NT syscall wrappers with PRNG-obfuscated tables; and an ever-growing shopping list of collection targets – 850 browser extensions alone consuming ~213 KB of binary space, like a burglar’s toolkit that takes up half the trunk. Two size epochs are visible – a ~160 KB jump around January 21 that marks a major feature release as clearly as a new coat of paint on an old building. The newest builds kept rolling off the line daily through February 2026, each one a little bigger, a little meaner, a little hungrier.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe loader chain: From dropper to stealer\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eTorg Grabber doesn’t show up at the party alone. It arrives wrapped in layers, the way a bad idea shows up wrapped in good intentions — three skins of encryption and obfuscation to peel away before the stealer ever touches memory. Each skin has a job. The outer one: crypter-level polymorphism, the kind that makes every sample look like a different animal. The middle: reflective PE loading, never leaving fingerprints on disk. The innermost: the stealer itself, finally naked and ready to work.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 0: The Dropper\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe outermost layer changes its face depending on who’s handing it out. Chromium-based browser that implements ABE registers its own Elevation Service COM class with a browser-specific CLSID. Extra methods in the interface push DecryptData to vtable offset +64 – method index 8 – instead of the standard +40, method index 5. Everyone else lines up neatly. Edge has to be different. Deep analysis in IDA Pro reveals these builds use a \u003cstrong\u003ecompletely different C2 backend\u003c/strong\u003e – \u003ccode\u003ePOST /api/machine/set-files-r\u003c/code\u003e with multipart form-data uploads – rather than Torg Grabber’s native REST API. Despite the different exfiltration protocol, the binaries share the full \u003ccode\u003eGRABBER_*\u003c/code\u003e environment variable framework, anti-VM/anti-CIS checks, and mutex pattern. They self-identify as \"\u003ccode\u003egrabber v1.0\u003c/code\u003e\" with plaintext debug messages in \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e. See \u003cem\u003eA Parallel Backend:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/em\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u003cem\u003e/api/machine/set-files-r\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/code\u003e for details.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e364e8168c3762cb784cdc65306447ebf\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u003cstrong\u003equick-neo.com\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/code\u003e, registered October 30, 2025, pre-dates all known samples by six weeks and carries a Cloudflare Radar rank of 10,000 and a Cisco Umbrella rank of ~242,000. IDA Pro analysis confirms its December 18 sample uses the same \u003ccode\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e/api/machine/set-files-r\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/code\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e backend\u003c/strong\u003e as the \u003ccode\u003e50elk.com\u003c/code\u003e builds – making it the earliest known sample with this protocol, from the same week the REST API transition began. See \u003cem\u003eA Parallel Backend:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/em\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u003cem\u003e/api/machine/set-files-r\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/code\u003e\u003cem\u003e \u003c/em\u003efor details.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe GRAB TCP server\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two experimental GRAB protocol domains –\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003ebk.tara.net.bd\u003c/code\u003e and\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eraketa.tara.net.bd\u003c/code\u003e – resolve to\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e190.92.174.248\u003c/code\u003e, hosted by \u003cstrong\u003eWHG Hosting Services Ltd\u003c/strong\u003e (AS199404, UK company, IP geolocated to India). Unlike the REST API domains, this one isn’t hiding behind Cloudflare. It uses a Let’s Encrypt wildcard for\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e*.tara.net.bd\u003c/code\u003e and shares the server with\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003epotshilpo.shop\u003c/code\u003e, a Bangladesh-based domain. Shared hosting. The developer phase, where you don’t yet have the budget or the paranoia for dedicated infrastructure. BitDefender and G-Data flag\u0026nbsp;tara.net.bd as malware. Someone noticed, even if not everyone.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat VT sees: Distribution and delivery\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eVirusTotal’s communicating files for the three busiest C2 domains tell the story of how Torg Grabber reaches its victims. The delivery mechanism is the oldest trick in the book, dressed up with a fresh coat of paint: trojanized installers, ZIP-packaged lures, digital candy left on the doorstep.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFantasy-themed lures\u003c/strong\u003e (\u003ccode\u003etechnologytorg.com\u003c/code\u003e): The ten most recent communicating files are 87–113 MB ZIP installers wearing auto-generated fantasy names like costumes at a masquerade:\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eAerovora.zip\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eNebulax.zip\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003ePyroxis.zip\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eIgnisyn.zip, Aetherion.zip\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eZephyrion.zip\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eAquathea.zip\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eNebulynx.zip\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eLuminavora.zip\u003c/code\u003e. The naming convention had the mechanical creativity of a builder script that read too many sci-fi paperbacks.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePirated software lures\u003c/strong\u003e (\u003ccode\u003egogenbydet.cc\u003c/code\u003e): Multiple 166 MB ZIPs labeled\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eTerraria_1.4.5.zip\u003c/code\u003e and\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eSetup.zip\u003c/code\u003e – bait for people who want something for nothing, which is most people. A smaller\u0026nbsp;loader \u003ccode\u003e.zip\u003c/code\u003e at 31 MB suggested a more targeted delivery chain, something with a shorter fuse.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eNumeric-ID lures\u003c/strong\u003e (\u003ccode\u003eattackzombie.com\u003c/code\u003e): Smaller 3 MB ZIPs with numeric filenames –\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e24323350\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e24353240\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e24427545\u003c/code\u003e – plus\u0026nbsp;installer \u003ccode\u003e.zip\u003c/code\u003e. A simpler naming scheme from the earliest REST API domain, back when the operation was still figuring out its wardrobe.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDroppers on disk\u003c/strong\u003e turn up as randomly-named executables in\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eC:\\Windows\\:\u0026nbsp;v4jkqh.exe\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003ehkjpy08.exe\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003ewe4osct.exe\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003ebfp2w6.exe\u003c/code\u003e. The naming pattern of a crypter or loader that doesn’t want to be remembered – digital aliases, disposable as matchbook covers.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eVT label noise\u003c/strong\u003e: The communicating files are variously tagged\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eTrojan.dacic/vidar\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003etrojan.aotera\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003etrojan. cryptagen\u003c/code\u003et, and even\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003etrojan.aotera/rhadamanthys\u003c/code\u003e – a mess of misattribution that brought Torg Grabber to our attention in the first place. None of these labels fit. They reflect the industry’s reflex of pinning unknown stealers to familiar family names, the way a police sketch ends up looking like everybody and nobody.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAll dedicated C2 domains share the same Cloudflare JARM fingerprint (\u003ccode\u003e27d40d40d00...525c\u003c/code\u003e) and use Google Trust Services TLS certificates. Matching suits. Same tailor.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttribution: Following the Telegram trail\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe 19 early Telegram-exfiltrating samples came with hardcoded bot tokens and channel IDs, carried openly in the binary like monogrammed luggage at an airport. By querying the Telegram Bot API, we obtained bot metadata, channel information, and – the prize – the \u003cstrong\u003eTelegram accounts of the channel creators\u003c/strong\u003e. The people who set up the drops. The hands behind the curtain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eSee Appendix A, Figure A5\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003e\u003cem\u003eThe Bots\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eBot\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eUsername\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStatus\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8247426625\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e@trtest12bot\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8284437818\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e@gr_bot_life_bot\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8456437646\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e@lalllaland_bot\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8471726820\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e@palayodd_bot\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e4 others\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cem\u003e(unknown)\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRevoked\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eFour bots still alive and ticking, tokens unrevoked, as of February 8, 2026. All eight channels private, two to three members each – the bot and one or two humans. Small operations. Tight circles. The kind of crew where everyone knows everyone and nobody knows anyone outside.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe Operators\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach exfiltration channel has a creator – the Telegram account that set up the room and invited the bot. We found four:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eUsername\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDisplay Name\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eUser ID\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eChannel\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNotable\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e@ChaChaGuru\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003ePope Robinson\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e7863185848\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e“BkChannel”\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eChaCha20 in the username\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e@OneCC_team\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eOneCC\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e7983438838\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e“playodd”\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eBio: “Selling CC\u0026gt;First hand\u0026gt;Sniffed”\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e@the_01G\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eX I\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e7960359642\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e“land”\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cem\u003e(none)\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLauriev Hontisorknnd\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8214631236\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e“1 kanal”\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e“1 kanal” = “channel 1” – the test run\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e@ChaChaGuru\u003c/strong\u003e was the lead we couldn’t ignore. The username is a direct reference to \u003cstrong\u003eChaCha20\u003c/strong\u003e – the stream cipher that runs through Torg Grabber’s veins like blood type. It encrypts the C2 payloads. It protects the GRAB protocol. It wraps the shellcode. And the “Bk” in “BkChannel” matches \u003ccode\u003ebk.tara.net.bd\u003c/code\u003e, the GRAB TCP domain. Two connections. Both pointing the same direction. This wasn’t circumstantial evidence; it was a man leaving his business card at the scene.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e@OneCC_team\u003c/strong\u003e presented as a group identity, and their public bio – “Selling CC\u0026gt;First hand\u0026gt;Sniffed” – was an open advertisement for stolen credit card sales. Not even coded. Just stated, plainly, the way a butcher advertises pork chops. Their profile photo history traced a career arc from an Amazon delivery truck through “OMG” and Bitcoin/AML imagery to a Guy Fawkes mask – a trajectory from e-commerce fraud through cryptocurrency crime to the anonymous hacking persona, documented in selfies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAll four creator accounts carry user IDs in the 7.8–8.2 billion range – created in the same timeframe, shortly before the bots themselves. The channel called “1 kanal” (Russian for “channel 1”) by “Lauriev Hontisorknnd” – a name as fabricated as a three-dollar bill – was the test channel. The first attempt. The rough draft.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat Torg Grabber steals\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe stealer’s appetite is broad, and it’s getting broader. It wants everything, and it’s not shy about asking.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHere’s the rap sheet:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6c94f8f1812a2022dd1d2f3d7da713fc\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBrowser credentials\u003c/strong\u003e: 25 Chromium-based browsers (Chrome, Edge, Brave, Opera, Vivaldi, Yandex, CocCoc, CryptoTab, and 17 others that never thought they’d end up on a list like this) and 8 Firefox-family browsers, with AES-GCM, DPAPI, and ABE support. Plus harvesting of credentials previously extracted by the \u003cstrong\u003e“Underground” tool\u003c/strong\u003e – a standalone credential extraction tool whose output Torg Grabber scavenges like a jackal following a lion (see below)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e189ced2b07dbbe824fef40f0391ad7f0\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBrowser extensions – 850 total\u003c/strong\u003e: Three hardcoded lists. \u003cstrong\u003e728 wallet extensions\u003c/strong\u003e covering every crypto wallet from MetaMask and Phantom down to ShibaWallet and xMetaCene. \u003cstrong\u003e103 password/2FA extensions\u003c/strong\u003e – LastPass, 1Password, Bitwarden, and every authenticator app that ever tried to keep someone safe. \u003cstrong\u003e19 notes extensions\u003c/strong\u003e, because sometimes people write their passwords on sticky notes, even digital ones. Full lists in the\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://github.com/gendigitalinc/ioc\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eIOC repository\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e902a0534ddd9db1b31ca1626e07310a9\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDesktop wallets\u003c/strong\u003e: 30+ standalone applications – Exodus, Atomic, Electrum, Monero, Daedalus. The whole menagerie\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e8584d6b90fd45056b2e40596641095c2\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eMessaging\u003c/strong\u003e: Discord tokens (LevelDB scanning), Telegram session data, Steam configs\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea137fd09ecd26e88aaf53686e2757bc6\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCredentials\u003c/strong\u003e: VPN configs from 8 providers, FTP clients (5), email (Thunderbird, Outlook), password managers (7), gaming platforms (6)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef207f85eab7a553a1507c70c0dd6959d\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eSystem profiling\u003c/strong\u003e: Hardware fingerprint, installed software, process list, screenshot, environment variables, and \u003cstrong\u003eAV detection\u003c/strong\u003e scanning 46 process signatures across 24 products – Kaspersky, Avast, ESET, CrowdStrike, SentinelOne, Windows Defender. It wants to know who’s watching before it starts working, the way a burglar checks for dogs before climbing the fence\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eaec55d241c9ae2b5988c1e458dd1ac8c\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFile grabbing\u003c/strong\u003e: Pattern-based collection from Desktop and Documents with C2-configurable rules\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5c1348e1d786ad029539f7c229d1606d\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eArbitrary code execution\u003c/strong\u003e: The C2 can deliver ChaCha-encrypted, zlib-compressed shellcode at registration. Just in case everything else wasn’t enough\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003e850 browser extensions: Casting the widest net\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003ePerhaps the most striking thing about Torg Grabber isn’t what it does, but how much of it there is. Eight hundred and fifty extension IDs, hardcoded into the binary, organized into three tidy categories like a catalog for a store that sells things that don’t belong to it:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCategory\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCount\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eArchive Path\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWhat They Steal\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWallet\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e728\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWallet/Extension/\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCrypto wallet seeds, keys, session data\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePassword/2FA\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e103\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003ePassword/\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003ePassword vaults, TOTP secrets, MFA tokens\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eNotes\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e19\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNotes/\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSaved notes – and the passwords people hide in them\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe \u003cstrong\u003e728 wallet extensions\u003c/strong\u003e cover essentially every crypto wallet ever conceived by human optimism. The marquee names are all there – MetaMask, Phantom, TrustWallet, Coinbase, Binance, Exodus, TronLink, Ronin, OKX, Keplr, Rabby, Sui, Solflare. But the list doesn’t stop at the big names. It keeps going, deep into the long tail, past projects with install counts you could fit in a phone booth – ShibaWallet, xMetaCene, Elli Sui Wallet. The kind of thoroughness that suggests the list was scraped from the Web Store rather than curated by hand. Nobody sits down and types 728 extension IDs one by one. Not even a criminal.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe \u003cstrong\u003e103 password extensions\u003c/strong\u003e are equally comprehensive. The majors – LastPass, 1Password, Bitwarden, KeePass, NordPass, Dashlane, ProtonPass, Enpass – plus niche products like Psono, Pleasant Password Server, and heylogin. And here’s the twist: \u003cstrong\u003e31 two-factor authentication extensions\u003c/strong\u003e are in the mix. 2FAAuth, GAuth, TOTP Authenticator, Akamai MFA. The stealer doesn’t just want the password. It wants the second factor too. The lock and the key and the deadbolt and the chain. Everything that stands between the victim and complete exposure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor each matched extension, the malware vacuums data from three locations: 1. \u003ccode\u003eLocal Extension Settings/\u003c/code\u003e – the extension’s LevelDB storage 2. \u003ccode\u003eIndexedDB/\u003c/code\u003e – any IndexedDB databases matching the extension ID 3. \u003ccode\u003eSync Extension Settings/\u003c/code\u003e – synced data, if the user has Chrome Sync enabled\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe archive paths include a DJB2 hash of the browser name – \u003ccode\u003eWallet/Extension/MetaMask/C-Chrome-a1b2c3d4/\u003c/code\u003e – to keep stolen credentials from different browsers filed separately. Organized crime, in the most literal sense.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe full extension lists are published in the IOC repository. Defenders and extension developers can use them. Everyone else can marvel at the ambition.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHarvesting a previous tool: “Underground”\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eEarly Torg Grabber builds contained code to harvest credentials from \u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\Underground\u003c/code\u003e – a path that, at first glance, had the look of an anti-detect browser’s home directory. We spent some time chasing that theory. Turns out we were looking at it wrong. The way you sometimes look at a shadow on the wall and see a man, but when the light shifts it’s just a coat on a hook.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat “underground” really is\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe recovered a number of\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\Underground\\logs.txt\u003c/code\u003e files from infected machines. The logs told a different story. “Underground” isn’t a browser at all. It’s a \u003cstrong\u003estandalone credential extraction tool\u003c/strong\u003e with a two-stage architecture that bears a family resemblance to Torg Grabber’s own ABE bypass – the kind of resemblance that makes you check if they had the same father:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eaebbd3d4aa7deb4f016350202282ff88\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHost process\u003c/strong\u003e (injector) – enumerates installed browsers, creates a suspended browser process, performs reflective DLL injection, and talks to the injected payload through named pipes\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0bdeb938b3bb7a840a901fe1addec28c\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInjected DLL\u003c/strong\u003e (reflective loader) – runs inside the browser process, decrypts the ABE-protected master key via Chrome’s COM Elevator service, then dumps credentials to JSON files\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe log output documents the full chain: browser enumeration, suspended process creation, ReflectiveLoader injection, named pipe setup (\u003ccode\u003e\\\\.\\pipe\\chrome.nacl.*,\u0026nbsp;\\\\.\\pipe\\mojo.*\u003c/code\u003e), ABE key decryption via COM, profile enumeration, and credential extraction. It writes its take as JSON files and plaintext token files to\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\Underground\u003c/code\u003e. Neat. Professional. The kind of work that leaves receipts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat Torg Grabber harvests\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen Torg Grabber finds this directory – and on machines where the operators have been running their toolkit, it will – it parses three types of JSON output files left behind by the Underground tool:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col start=\"3\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2607892443ff28f869bd1fa195fa5a42\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePasswords\u003c/strong\u003e –\u0026nbsp;{\u003ccode\u003e\"origin\":\"...\"\u003c/code\u003e} objects with URLs, usernames, and passwords\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0281f0bdf9f4fa2e26ba0e5c099a8086\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCookies\u003c/strong\u003e –\u0026nbsp;{\u003ccode\u003e\"domain\":\"...\"\u003c/code\u003e} objects with full cookie metadata\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb155f96476575ab11879fc30506733c4\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePayment cards\u003c/strong\u003e –\u0026nbsp;{\u003ccode\u003e\"card_number\":\"...\"\u003c/code\u003e} objects with card details\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt also collects three token files (\u003ccode\u003egaming_tokens.txt\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003emedia_tokens.txt\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003esocial_tokens.txt\u003c/code\u003e) containing session tokens for various platforms. Double-dipping. Credentials that were already extracted once, stolen again for the archive.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe connection\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe architectural overlap between Underground and Torg Grabber is the kind of thing that makes you sit up in your chair. Both use reflective DLL injection into suspended browser processes. Both use named pipe IPC. Both do COM-based ABE bypass. The blueprints are close enough to be cousins.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBut the DLL-side log strings are completely different – Torg Grabber’s\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eabe_decrypt.dll\u003c/code\u003e uses\u0026nbsp;[\u003ccode\u003eDLL\u003c/code\u003e] prefixed messages that don’t appear anywhere in Underground’s logs, and the host-side injector strings from Underground are absent from\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eabe_decrypt.dll\u003c/code\u003e. Different binaries. Same school of thought. This rules out a shared executable but points toward a shared codebase, a common author, or tools from the same ecosystem – bought from the same shelf in the same underground marketplace.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe working hypothesis: the operators who deploy Torg Grabber also ran the Underground tool on infected machines – either as a precursor or running alongside. Torg Grabber’s JSON parsers then scavenge whatever Underground left behind, like a second shift coming in to clean out what the first shift missed. The machine_api line tracked the main Torg evolution: ABE bypass added, env var framework integrated, binary nearly tripled in size. Not a dead fork. Actively maintained. The backend protocol itself is modular and swappable. The face changes. The fingerprints don’t.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eConclusion\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eTorg Grabber is a case study in velocity. Three months from prototype to production. Four exfiltration mechanisms tried – Telegram Bot API, GRAB TCP, REST API, and the parallel \u003ccode\u003e/api/machine/set-files-r backend\u003c/code\u003e – each one a rung on a ladder that only goes up. Eighteen C2 domains across eight registrars. A binary that nearly tripled in size while its developers were still registering the domains it would phone home to. It was like watching someone build an airplane while it was already taxiing down the runway.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Telegram breadcrumbs left behind by the early builds – bot tokens, channel metadata, and an operator whose username literally namedrops the malware’s signature encryption algorithm – provided a window into the human side of the operation. These aren’t ghosts. They’re people, with Telegram Premium subscriptions and public bios that read like job applications for a criminal enterprise.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy resolving the numeric operator tags through OSINT databases, we put faces to eight of twelve MaaS customers. The faces weren’t pretty. Usernames referencing scamming (@SCAMHERE1, @scamsomebody), victims (\u003ccode\u003e@mamontovv\u003c/code\u003e, from “мамонт” – the mammoth, the mark, the one who showed up), suckers (\u003ccode\u003e@lox3526tyrk\u003c/code\u003e, from “лох” – the word speaks for itself), and stolen-credential marketplaces (\u003ccode\u003e@scarface_logs\u003c/code\u003e linking to \u003ccode\u003e@NoMetaRu\u003c/code\u003e). Four carry Telegram Premium – established actors, not disposable identities. Combined with the developer accounts recovered from the bot tokens, the picture is unusually complete. Both sides of a MaaS operation, buyer and seller, laid out under the light.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe observed delivery chain tells its own story – from a ClickFix clipboard attack hosted on Google Apps Script, through a multi-stage PowerShell stager with a fake progress bar and XOR-encrypted BITS transfers, to three polymorphic MinGW loaders served from disposable .pw domains. The delivery infrastructure is as carefully engineered as the stealer itself. Strict role separation between domains – stagers on j0o.pw, encrypted loaders on t4e.pw/re3.pw, exfiltration on the main C2 cluster – provides operational resilience: burning one domain doesn’t touch the others. The use of trusted platforms (Google Apps Script, Dropbox) for initial access and server-side polymorphism for loader generation makes static blocking about as effective as putting up a “No Trespassing” sign at a public park.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe family remains under active development. New C2 domains registered weekly. Builds compiled daily – 334 samples and counting, each one a fresh fingerprint. The operator base growing, 40+ tags and rising, new customers walking through the door. What initially looked like a three-day experiment with environment-variable configuration has since become the standard: every build from Feb 10 onward ships with \u003ccode\u003eGRABBER_*\u003c/code\u003e env var support and compiler-inlined string assignments, replacing the earlier plaintext \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e approach. The most significant operational shift is the \u003cstrong\u003edomain rotation chain\u003c/strong\u003e (\u003ccode\u003egogenbydet.cc\u003c/code\u003e → \u003ccode\u003ebbcplay.top\u003c/code\u003e → \u003ccode\u003eplaybergs.info\u003c/code\u003e), where entire operator groups migrate to fresh infrastructure roughly every ten days – a cadence that makes domain-based blocking a losing game. Meanwhile, \u003ccode\u003eevasivestars.com\u003c/code\u003e has become a mass-build factory with date-encoded batch tags, suggesting automated deployment pipelines. We’ll keep watching. These are the kind of people who don’t stop until someone stops
